[[Longueville]]<br>Did never [[Sonnet|sonnet]] for her sake compile,<br>Nor never lay his wreathed arms athwart<br>His loving bosom to keep down his heart?<br>I have been closely shrouded in this bush,<br>And marked you both, and for you both did blush.<br>I heard your guilty rhymes, observed your fashion,<br>Saw sighs reek from you, noted [[Well|well]] your passion.<br>‘Ay me!’ says one, ‘O jovel’ the other cries.<br>One, her hairs were gold; crystal the other’s eyes.<br>(To [[Longueville]]) You would for paradise break [[Faith|faith]] and troth,<br>(To [[Dumaine]]) And Jove for your [[Love|love]] would infringe an oath.<br>What will Biron say when that he shall hear<br>[[Faith]] so infringed, which such zeal did swear?<br>How will he scorn, how will he spend his wit!<br>How will he triumph, leap, and laugh at it!<br>For all the wealth that ever I did see<br>I would not have him know so much by me. |
| BIRON | (coming forward)<br>Now step I forth to whip hypocrisy.<br>Ah, good my liege, I pray thee pardon me.<br>Good heart, what grace hast thou thus to reprove<br>These worms for loving, that art most in [[Love|love]]?<br>Your eyes do make no coaches. In your [[Tears|tears]]<br>There is no certain princess that appears.<br>You’ll not be perjured, ‘tis a hateful thing;<br>Tush, none but minstrels like of sonneting!<br>But are you not ashamed, nay, are you not,<br>All three of you, to be thus much o’ershot?<br>(To [[Longueville]]) You found his mote, the [[King]] your mote did see,<br>But I a beam do find in each of three.<br>O, what a scene of [[Fool|fool]]’ry have I seen,<br>Of sighs, of groans, of sorrow, and of teen!<br>O me, with what strict patience have I sat,<br>To see a [[King|king]] transformed to a gnat!<br>To see great Hercules whipping a gig,<br>And profound Solomon to tune a jig,<br>And Nestor play at pushpin with the boys,<br>And critic [[Timon]] laugh at idle toys!<br>Where lies thy grief, O tell me, good [[Dumaine]]?<br>And, gentle [[Longueville]], where lies thy [[Pain|pain]]?<br>And where my liege’s? All about the breast.<br>A caudle, ho! |
| [[King|KING]] | Too bitter is thy jest.<br>Are we betrayed thus to thy over-view? |
| BIRON | Not you to me, but I betrayed by you.<br>I that am honest, I that hold it sin<br>To break the vow I am engaged in.<br>I am betrayed by keeping company<br>With men like you, men of inconstancy.<br>When shall you see me write a thing in rhyme,<br>Or groan for Joan, or spend a minute’s [[Time|time]]<br>In pruning me? When shall you hear that I<br>Will praise a hand, a foot, a face, an eye,<br>A gait, a state, a brow, a breast, a waist,<br>A leg, a limb? |
| [[King|KING]] | Soft, whither away so fast?<br>A true man or a thief, that gallops so? |
| BIRON | I post from [[Love|love]]; good [[Lover|lover]], let me go. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter aquenetta with a letter, and [[Costard]] the clown |
| JAQUENETTA | God bless the [[King]]! |
| [[King|KING]] | What present hast thou there? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Some certain treason. |
| [[King|KING]] | What makes treason here? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Nay, it makes nothing, sir. |
| [[King|KING]] | If it mar nothing neither,<br>The treason and you go in peace away together! |
| JAQUENETTA | I beseech your grace, let this letter be read.<br>Our parson misdoubts it; ’twas treason, he said. |
| [[King|KING]] | Biron, read it over.<br>Biron takes and reads the letter<br>(To Jaquenetta) Where hadst thou it? |
| JAQUENETTA | Of [[Costard]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | (to [[Costard]]) Where hadst thou it? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Of Dun Adramadio, Dun Adramadio.<br>Biron [[Tears|tears]] the letter |
| [[King|KING]] | (to Biron)<br>How now, what is in you? Why dost thou tear it? |
| BIRON | A toy, my liege, a toy. Your grace needs not [[Fear|fear]] it. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | It did move him to passion, and therefore let’s hear it. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | (taking up a piece of the letter)<br>It is Biron’s writing, and here is his name. |
| BIRON | (to [[Costard]])<br>Ah, you whoreson loggerhead, you were born to do<br>me shamel<br>Guilty, my lord, guilty! I confess, I confess. |
| [[King|KING]] | What? |
| BIRON | That you three fools lacked me [[Fool|fool]] to make up the<br>mess.<br>He, he, and you-e’en you, my liege-and I<br>Are pickpurses in [[Love|love]], and we deserve to die.<br>O, dismiss this audience, and I shall tell you more. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Now the number is even. |
| BIRON | True, true; we are four.<br>Will these turtles be gone? |
| [[King|KING]] | Hence, sirs; away. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Walk aside the true folk, and let the traitors stay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Costard]] and Jaquenetta |
| BIRON | Sweet lords, sweet tovers!—O, let us embrace.<br>As true we are as flesh and [[Blood|blood]] can be.<br>The sea will ebb and flow, heaven show his face.<br>Young [[Blood|blood]] doth not obey an old decree.<br>We cannot [[Cross|cross]] the cause why we were born,<br>Therefore of all hands must we be forsworn. |
| [[King|KING]] | What, did these rent lines show some [[Love|love]] of thine? |
| BIRON | ‘Did they’, quoth you? Who sees the heavenly [[Rosaline]]<br>That, like a rude and savage man of Ind<br>At the first op’ning of the gorgeous east,<br>Bows not his vassal head and, strucken blind,<br>Kisses the base ground with obedient breast?<br>What peremptory eagle-sighted eye<br>Dares look upon the heaven of her brow<br>That is not blinded by her majesty? |
| [[King|KING]] | What zeal, what fury hath inspired thee now?<br>My [[Love|love]], her mistress, is a gracious [[Moon|moon]],<br>She an attending star, scarce seen a light. |
| BIRON | My eyes are then no eyes, nor I Biron.<br>O, but for my [[Love|love]], day would turn to [[Night|night]].<br>Of all complexions the culled sovereignty<br>Do meet as at a fair in her fair cheek,<br>Where several worthies make one dignity,<br>Where nothing wants that want itself doth seek.<br>Lend me the flourish of all gentle tongues—<br>Fie, painted rhetoric! O, she needs it not.<br>To things of sale a seller’s praise belongs.<br>She passes praise—then praise too short doth blot.<br>A withered hermit fivescore winters worn<br>Might shake off fifty, looking in her eye.<br>[[Beauty]] doth varnish age as if new-born,<br>And gives the crutch the cradle’s infancy.<br>O, ’tis the sun that maketh all things shine. |
| [[King|KING]] | By heaven, thy [[Love|love]] is black as ebony. |
| BIRON | Is ebony like her? O word divine!<br>A wife of such wood were felicity.<br>O, who can give an oath? Where is a book,<br>That I may swear [[Beauty|beauty]] doth [[Beauty|beauty]] lack<br>If that she learn not of her eye to look?<br>No face is fair that is not full so black. |
| [[King|KING]] | O [[Paradox|paradox]] ! Black is the badge of hell,<br>The hue of dungeons and the style of [[Night|night]],<br>And [[Beauty|beauty]]’s crest becomes the heavens [[Well|well]]. |
| BIRON | Devils soonest tempt, resembling spirits of light.<br>O, if in black my lady’s brows be decked,<br>It mourns that painting and usurping hair<br>Should ravish doters with a false aspect,<br>And therefore is she born to make black fair.<br>Her favour turns the fashion of the days,<br>For native [[Blood|blood]] is counted painting now,<br>And therefore red that would avoid dispraise<br>Paints itself black to imitate her brow. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | To look like her are chimney-sweepers black. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | And since her [[Time|time]] are colliers counted bright. |
| [[King|KING]] | And Ethiops of their sweet complexion crack. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Dark needs no candles now, for dark is light. |
| BIRON | Your mistresses dare never come in rain,<br>For [[Fear|fear]] their colours should be washed away. |
| [[King|KING]] | ‘Twere good yours did; for, sir, to tell you plain,<br>I’ll find a fairer face not washed today. |
| BIRON | I’ll prove her fair, or talk till doomsday here. |
| [[King|KING]] | No devil will fright thee then so much as she. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | I never knew man hold vile stuff so dear. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | (showing his foot)<br>Look, here’s thy [[Love|love]]—my foot and her face see. |
| BIRON | O, if the streets were paved with thine eyes<br>Her feet were much too dainty for such tread. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | O vile! Then as she goes, what upward lies<br>The street should see as she walked overhead. |
| [[King|KING]] | But what of this? Are we not all in [[Love|love]]? |
| BIRON | Nothing so sure, and thereby all forsworn. |
| [[King|KING]] | Then leave this chat and, good Biron, now prove<br>Our loving lawful and our [[Faith|faith]] not torn. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Ay, marry there, some flattery for this evil. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | O, some authority how to proceed,<br>Some tricks, some quillets how to cheat the devil. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Some salve for perjury. |
| BIRON | O, ‘tis more than need.<br>Have at you, then, affection’s men-at-arms.<br>Consider what you first did swear unto:<br>To fast, to study, and to see no woman—<br>Flat treason ’gainst the kingly state of youth.<br>Say, can you fast? Your stomachs are too young,<br>And abstinence engenders maladies.<br>O, we have made a vow to study, lords,<br>And in that vow we have forsworn our books;<br>For when would you, my liege, or you, or you<br>In leaden contemplation have found out<br>Such fiery numbers as the prompting eyes<br>Of [[Beauty|beauty]]’s tutors have enriched you with?<br>Other slow arts entirely keep the brain,<br>And therefore, finding barren practisers,<br>Scarce show a harvest of their heavy toil.<br>But [[Love|love]], first learned in a lady’s eyes,<br>Lives not [[Alone|alone]] immured in the brain,<br>But with the motion of all elements<br>Courses as swift as thought in every power,<br>And gives to every power a [[Double|double]] power<br>Above their functions and their offices.<br>It adds a precious seeing to the eye—<br>A [[Lover|lover]]’s eyes will gaze an eagle blind.<br>A [[Lover|lover]]’s ear will hear the lowest sound<br>When the suspicious head of theft is stopped.<br>[[Love]]’s feeling is more soft and sensible<br>Than are the tender horns of cockled snails.<br>[[Love]]’s tongue proves dainty Bacchus gross in taste.<br>For valour, is not [[Love|love]] a Hercules,<br>Still climbing trees in the Hesperides?<br>Subtle as Sphinx, as sweet and musical<br>As bright [[Apollo]]’s lute strung with his hair;<br>And when [[Love|love]] speaks, the voice of all the gods<br>Make heaven drowsy with the harmony.<br>Never durst poet touch a pen to write<br>Until his ink were tempered with [[Love|love]]’s sighs.<br>O, then his lines would ravish savage ears,<br>And plant in tyrants mild [[Humility|humility]].<br>From women’s eyes this doctrine I derive.<br>They sparkle still the right Promethean [[Fire|fire]].<br>They are the books, the arts, the academes<br>That show, contain, and nourish all the world,<br>Else none at all in aught proves excellent.<br>Then fools you were these women to forswear,<br>Or keeping what is sworn, you will prove fools.<br>For wisdom’s sake—a word that all men [[Love|love]]—<br>Or for [[Love|love]]’s sake—a word that loves all men—<br>Or for men’s sake—the authors of these women—<br>Or women’s sake—by whom we men are men—<br>Let us once lose our oaths to find ourselves,<br>Or else we lose ourselves to keep our oaths.<br>It is religion to be thus forsworn,<br>For [[Charity|charity]] itself fulfils the law,<br>And who can sever [[Love|love]] from [[Charity|charity]]? |
| [[King|KING]] | Saint Cupid, then, and, soldiers, to the field! |
| BIRON | Advance your standards, and upon them, lords.<br>Pell-mell, down with them; but be first advised<br>In conflict that you get the sun of them. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | Now to plain dealing. Lay these glozes by.<br>Shall we resolve to woo these girls of France? |
| [[King|KING]] | And win them, too! Therefore let us devise<br>Some entertainment for them in their tents. |
| BIRON | First, from the park let us conduct them thither;<br>Then homeward every man attach the hand<br>Of his fair mistress. In the afternoon<br>We will with some strange pastime solace them,<br>Such as the shortness of the [[Time|time]] can shape,<br>For revels, dances, masques, and merry hours<br>Forerun fair [[Love|love]], strewing her way with [[Flowers|flowers]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | Away, away, no [[Time|time]] shall be omitted<br>That will be [[Time|time]], and may by us be fitted. |
| BIRON | Allons, allons! Sowed cockle reaped no corn,<br>And justice always whirls in equal measure.<br>Light wenches may prove plagues to men forsworn.<br>If so, our copper buys no better treasure. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Holofernes the pedant, Nathaniel the curate, and Anthony Dull |
| HOLOFERNES | Satis quid sufficit. |
| NATHANIEL | I praise Good for you, sir. Your reasons at dinner have been sharp and sententious, pleasant without scurrility, witty without affections, audacious without impudency, learned without opinion, and strange without heresy. I did converse this quondam day with a companion of the [[King]]’s who is intituled, nominated, or called Don Adriano de Armado. |
| HOLOFERNES | Novi hominum tanquam te. His [[Humour|humour]] is lofty, his discourse peremptory, his tongue filed, his eye ambitious, his gait majestical, and his general behaviour vain, ridiculous, and thrasonical. He is too picked, too spruce, too affected, too odd, as it were, too peregrinate, as I may call it. |
| NATHANIEL | A most singular and choice epithet.<br>He draws out his table-book |
| HOLOFERNES | He draweth out the thread of his verbosity finer than the staple of his argument. I abhor such fanatical phantasims, such insociable and point-device companions, such rackers of orthography as to speak ‘dout’, sine ‘b’, when he should say ‘[[Doubt|doubt]]’; ‘det’ when he should pronounce ‘debt’—‘d, e, b, t’, not ‘d, e, t’. He clepeth a calf ‘caul’, half ‘haul’, neighbour vocatur ‘nebour’—‘neigh’ abbreviated ‘ne’. This is abhominaMe—which he would call ‘abominable’. It insinuateth me of insanire—ne intelligis, domine?—to make frantic, lunatic. |
| NATHANIEL | Laus deo, bone intelligo. |
| HOLOFERNES | Bone? Bon, fort bon—Priscian a little scratched-‘twill serve. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado the braggart, Mote his boy, and [[Costard]] the clown |
| NATHANIEL | Videsne quis venit?<br>xoLOFeRrrss Video, et gaudio. |
| ARMADO | (to Mote) Chirrah.<br>xoLOVExNES (to Nathaniel) Quare ‘chirrah’, not ‘sirrah’? |
| ARMADO | Men of peace, [[Well|well]] encountered. |
| HOLOFERNES | Most military sir, salutation! |
| MOTE | (aside to [[Costard]]) They have been at a great feast of languages and stolen the scraps. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | (aside to Mote) O, they have lived long on the alms-basket of words. I marvel thy master hath not eaten thee for a word, for thou art not so long by the head as honorificabilitudinitatibus. Thou art easier swallowed than a flapdragon. |
| MOTE | (aside to [[Costard]]) Peace, the peal begins. |
| ARMADO | (to Holofernes) Monsieur, are you not lettered? |
| MOTE | Yes, yes, he teaches boys the horn-book. What is ‘a, b’ spelled backward, with the horn on his head? |
| HOLOFERNES | Ba, pueritia, with a horn added. |
| MOTE | Ba, most silly sheep, with a horn! You hear his learning. |
| HOLOFERNES | Quis, quis, thou consonant? |
| MOTE | The last of the five vowels if you repeat them, or the fifth if I. |
| HOLOFERNES | I will repeat them: a, e, i— |
| MOTE | The sheep. The other two concludes it: o, u. |
| ARMADO | Now by the salt wave of the Mediterraneum a sweet touch, a quick venue of wit; snip, snap, quick, and [[Home|home]]. It rejoiceth my intellect—true wit. |
| MOTE | Offered by a [[Child|child]] to an old man, which is ‘wit-old’. |
| HOLOFERNES | What is the figure? What is the figure? |
| MOTE | Horns. |
| HOLOFERNES | Thou disputes like an infant. Go whip thy gig. |
| MOTE | Lend me your horn to make one, and I will whip about your infamy circum circa—a gig of a cuckold’s horn. |
| CUSTARD | An I had but one penny in the world, thou shouldst have it to buy gingerbread. (Giving money) Hold, there is the very remuneration I had of thy master, thou halfpenny purse of wit, thou pigeon-egg of discretion. O, an the heavens were so pleased that thou wert but my bastard, what a joyful father wouldst thou make me! Go to, thou hast it ad dunghill, at the fingers’ ends, as they say. |
| HOLOFERNES | O, I smell false Latin—‘dunghill’ for unguem. |
| ARMADO | Arts-man, preambulate. We will be singled from the barbarous. Do you not educate youth at the charge-[[house]] on the top of the mountain? |
| HOLOFERNES | Or mons, the [[Hill|hill]]. |
| ARMADO | At your sweet pleasure, for the mountain. |
| HOLOFERNES | I do, sans question. |
| ARMADO | Sir, it is the [[King]]’s most sweet pleasure and affection to congratulate the Princess at her pavilion in the posteriors of this day, which the rude multitude call the afternoon. |
| HOLOFERNES | The posterior of the day, most generous sir, is liable, congruent, and measurable for the afternoon. The word is [[Well|well]] culled, choice, sweet, and apt, I do assure you, sir, I do assure. |
| ARMADO | Sir, the [[King]] is a noble gentleman, and my familiar, I do assure ye, very good friend. For what is inward between us, let it pass. I do beseech thee, remember thy courtesy. I beseech thee, apparel thy head. And, among other important and most serious designs, and of great import indeed, too—but let that pass, for I must tell thee it will please his grace, by the world, sometime to lean upon my poor shoulder and with his royal finger thus dally with my excrement, with my mustachio. But, sweetheart, let that pass. By the world, I recount no [[Fable|fable]]. Some certain special honours it pleaseth his [[Greatness|greatness]] to impart to Armado, a soldier, a man of travel, that hath seen the world. But let that pass. The very all of all is—but, sweetheart, I do implore secrecy—that the [[King]] would have me present the Princess-sweet chuck-with some delightful ostentation, or show, or pageant, or antic, or firework. Now, understanding that the curate and your sweet [[Self|self]] are good at such eruptions and sudden breaking-out of mirth, as it were, I have acquainted you withal to the end to crave your assistance. |
| HOLOFERNES | Sir, you shall present before her the Nine Worthies. Sir Nathaniel, as concerning some entertainment of [[Time|time]], some show in the posterior of this day to be rendered by our assistance, the [[King]]’s command, and this most gallant, illustrate, and learned gentleman before the Princess, I say none so fit as to present the Nine Worthies. |
| NATHANIEL | Where will you find men worthy enough to present them? |
| HOLOFERNES | Joshua, yourself; myself, Judas Maccabeus; and this gallant gentleman, [[Hector]]. This swain, because of his great limb or joint, shall pass [[Pompey]] the Great; the page, Hercules. |
| ARMADO | Pardon, sir, error! He is not quantity enough for that Worthy’s thumb. He is not so big as the end of his club. |
| HOLOFERNES | Shall I have audience? He shall present Hercules in minority. His enter and exit shall be strangling a snake, and I will have an apology for that purpose. |
| MOTE | An excellent device! So, if any of the audience hiss, you may cry ‘[[Well]] done, Hercules, now thou crushest the snake!’—that is the way to make an offence gracious, though few have the grace to do it. |
| ARMADO | For the rest of the Worthies? |
| HOLOFERNES | I will play three myself. |
| MOTE | Thrice-worthy gentleman! |
| ARMADO | Shall I tell you a thing? |
| HOLOFERNES | We attend. |
| ARMADO | We will have, if this fadge not, an antic. I beseech you, follow. |
| HOLOFERNES | Via, goodman Dull! Thou hast spoken no word all this while. |
| DULL | Nor understood none neither, sir. |
| HOLOFERNES | Allons! We will employ thee. |
| DULL | I’ll make one in a [[Dance|dance]] or so, or I will play on the tabor to the Worthies, and let them [[Dance|dance]] the hay. |
| HOLOPERNES | Most dull, honest Dull! To our sport, away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Princess and her ladies: [[Rosaline]], [[Maria]], and Catherine |
| PRINCESS | Sweethearts, we shall be rich ere we depart,<br>If fairings come thus plentifully in.<br>A lady walled about with diamonds—<br>Look you what I have from the loving [[King]]. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Madam, came nothing else along with that? |
| PRINCESS | Nothing but this?—yes, as much [[Love|love]] in rhyme<br>As would be crammed up in a sheet of paper<br>Writ o’ both sides the leaf, margin and all,<br>That he was fain to seal on Cupid’s name. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | That was the way to make his godhead wax,<br>For he hath been five thousand year a boy. |
| CATHERINE | Ay, and a shrewd unhappy gallows, too. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | You’ll ne’er be friends with him, a killed your sister. |
| CATHERINE | He made her [[Melancholy|melancholy]], sad, and heavy,<br>And so she died. Had she been light like you,<br>Of such a merry, nimble, stirring spirit,<br>She might ha’ been a grandam ere she died;<br>And so may you, for a light heart lives long. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | What’s your dark meaning, mouse, of this light word? |
| CATHERINE | A light condition in a [[Beauty|beauty]] dark. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | We need more light to find your meaning out. |
| CATHERINE | You’ll mar the light by taking it in snuff,<br>Therefore I’ll darkly end the argument. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Look what you do, you do it still i’th’ dark. |
| CATHERINE | So do not you, for you are a light wench. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Indeed I weigh not you, and therefore light. |
| CATHERINE | You weigh me not? O, that’s you care not for me. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Great reason, for past care is still past cure. |
| PRINCESS | [[Well]] bandied, both; a set of wit [[Well|well]] played.<br>But [[Rosaline]], you have a favour, too.<br>Who sent it? And what is it? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | I would you knew.<br>An if my face were but as fair as yours<br>My favour were as great, be witness this.<br>Nay, I have verses, too, I thank Biron,<br>The numbers true, and were the numb’ring, too,<br>I were the fairest goddess on the ground.<br>I am compared to twenty thousand fairs.<br>O, he hath drawn my picture in his letter. |
| PRINCESS | Anything like? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Much in the letters, nothing in the praise. |
| PRINCESS | Beauteous as ink—a good conclusion. |
| CATHERINE | Fair as a text B in a copy-book. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Ware pencils, ho! Let me not die your debtor,<br>My red dominical, my golden letter.<br>O, that your face were not so full of O’s ! |
| PRINCESS | A pox of that jest; I beshrew all shrews.<br>But Catherine, what was sent to you from fair [[Dumaine]] ? |
| CATHERINE | Madam, this glove. |
| PRINCESS | Did he not send you twain ? |
| CATHERINE | Yes, madam; and moreover,<br>Some thousand verses of a faithful [[Lover|lover]].<br>A huge [[Translation|translation]] of hypocrisy<br>Vilely compiled, profound simplicity. |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | This and these pearls to me sent [[Longueville]].<br>The letter is too long by half a mile. |
| PRINCESS | I think no less. Dost thou not wish in heart<br>The chain were longer and the letter short? |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | Ay, or I would these hands might never part. |
| PRINCESS | We are wise girls to mock our [[lovers]] so. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | They are worse fools to purchase mocking so.<br>That same Biron I’ll torture ere I go.<br>O that I knew he were but in by th’ week!—<br>How I would make him fawn, and beg, and seek,<br>And wait the season, and observe the times,<br>And spend his prodigal wits in bootless rhymes,<br>And shape his service wholly to my hests,<br>And make him proud to make me proud that jests!<br>So pursuivant-like would I o’ersway his state<br>That he should be my [[Fool|fool]], and I his fate. |
| PRINCESS | None are so surely caught when they are catched<br>As wit turned [[Fool|fool]]. Folly in wisdom hatched<br>Hath wisdom’s warrant, and the help of school,<br>And wit’s own grace, to grace a learned [[Fool|fool]]. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | The [[Blood|blood]] of youth burns not with such excess<br>As gravity’s revolt to wantonness. |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | Folly in fools bears not so strong a note<br>As [[Fool|fool]]’ry in the wise when wit doth dote,<br>Since all the power thereof it doth apply<br>To prove, by wit, worth in simplicity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Boyet |
| PRINCESS | Here comes Boyet, and mirth is in his face. |
| BOYET | O, I am stabbed with laughter! Where’s her grace? |
| PRINCESS | Thy news, Boyet? |
| BOYET | Prepare, madam, prepare.<br>Arm, wenches, arm. Encounters mounted are<br>Against your peace. [[Love]] doth approach disguised,<br>Armed in arguments. You’ll be surprised.<br>Muster your wits, stand in your own defence,<br>Or hide your heads like cowards and fly hence. |
| PRINCESS | Saint Denis to Saint Cupid! What are they<br>That charge their breath against us? Say, scout, say. |
| BOYET | Under the cool shade of a sycamore<br>I thought to close mine eyes some half an hour<br>When lo, to interrupt my purposed rest<br>Toward that shade I might behold addressed<br>The [[King]] and his companions. Warily<br>I stole into a neighbour thicket by<br>And overheard what you shall overhear:<br>That by and by disguised they will be here.<br>Their herald is a pretty knavish page<br>That [[Well|well]] by heart hath conned his embassage.<br>Action and accent did they teach him there.<br>‘Thus must thou speak’, and ‘thus thy [[Body|body]] bear’.<br>And ever and anon they made a [[Doubt|doubt]]<br>Presence majestical would put him out,<br>‘For’, quoth the [[King]], ‘an angel shalt thou see,<br>Yet [[Fear|fear]] not thou, but speak audaciously.’<br>The boy replied, ‘An angel is not evil.<br>I should have feared her had she been a devil.’<br>With that all laughed and clapped him on the<br>shoulder,<br>Making the bold wag by their praises bolder.<br>One rubbed his elbow thus, and fleered, and swore<br>A better speech was never spoke before.<br>Another with his finger and his thumb<br>Cried ‘Via, we will do’t, come what will come!’<br>The third he capered and cried ‘All goes welll’<br>The fourth turned on the toe and down he fell.<br>With that they all did tumble on the ground<br>With such a zealous laughter, so profound,<br>That in this spleen ridiculous appears,<br>To check their folly, passion’s solemn [[Tears|tears]]. |
| PRINCESS | But what, but what—come they to visit us? |
| BOYET | They do, they do, and are apparelled thus |
| *(didascalia)* |  |
| BOYET | Like Muscovites or Russians, as I guess.<br>Their purpose is to parley, to [[Court|court]] and [[Dance|dance]],<br>And every one his [[Love|love]]-suit will advance<br>Unto his several mistress, which they’ll know<br>By favours several which they did bestow. |
| PRINCESS | And will they so ? The gallants shall be tasked,<br>For, ladies, we will every one be masked,<br>And not a man of them shall have the grace,<br>Despite of suit, to see a lady’s face.<br>(To [[Rosaline]]) Hold, take thou this, my sweet, and give me thine.<br>So shall Biron take me for [[Rosaline]].<br>She changes favours with [[Rosaline]]<br>(To Catherine and [[Maria]])<br><br>And change you favours, too. So shall your loves<br>Woo contrary, deceived by these removes.<br>Catherine and [[Maria]] change favours |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Come on, then, wear the favours most in sight. |
| CATHERINE | But in this changing what is your intent? |
| PRINCESS | The effect of my intent is to [[Cross|cross]] theirs.<br>They do it but in mockery-merriment,<br>And mock for mock is only my intent.<br>Their several counsels they unbosom shall<br>To loves mistook, and so be mocked withal<br>Upon the next occasion that we meet<br>With visages displayed to talk and greet. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | But shall we [[Dance|dance]] if they [[Desire|desire]] us to’t? |
| PRINCESS | No, to the [[Death|death]] we will not move a foot,<br>Nor to their penned speech render we no grace,<br>But while ’tis spoke each turn away her face. |
| BOYET | Why, that contempt will kill the speaker’s heart,<br>And quite [[divorce]] his [[Memory|memory]] from his part. |
| PRINCESS | Therefore I do it; and I make no [[Doubt|doubt]]<br>The rest will ne‘er come in if he be out.<br>There’s no such sport as sport by sport o’erthrown,<br>To make theirs ours, and ours none but our own.<br>So shall we stay, mocking intended game,<br>And they [[Well|well]] mocked depart away with shame.<br>A trumpet sounds |
| BOYET | The trumpet sounds, be masked, the masquers come.<br>The ladies mask. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter blackamoors with music; the boy Mote with |
| BOYET | a speech ; the [[King]] and his lords, disguised as<br>Russians |
| MOTE | All hail, the richest beauties on the earth! |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Beauties no richer than rich taffeta. |
| MOTE | A holy parcel of the fairest dames—<br>The ladies turn their backs to him<br>That ever turned their—backs to mortal views. |
| BIRON | ‘Their eyes’, [[Villain|villain]], ‘their eyes’! |
| MOTE | That ever turned their eyes to mortal views.<br>Out ... |
| BOYET | True, out indeed! |
| MOTE | Out of your favours, heavenly spirits, vouchsafe<br>Not to behold— |
| BIRON | ‘Once to behold’, rogue! |
| MOTE | Once to behold with your sun-beamèd eyes—<br>With your sun-beamèd eyes— |
| BOYET | They will not answer to that epithet.<br>You were best call it ‘daughter-beamèd’ eyes. |
| MOTE | They do not mark me, and that brings me out. |
| BIRON | Is this your perfectness? Be gone, you rogue! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Mote |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | (as the Princess)<br>What would these strangers ? Know their minds, Boyet.<br>If they do speak our language, ‘tis our will<br>That some plain man recount their purposes.<br>Know what they would. |
| BOYET | What would you with the Princess? |
| BIRON | Nothing but peace and gentle visitation. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | What would they, say they? |
| BOYET | Nothing but peace and gentle visitation. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Why, that they have, and bid them so be gone. |
| BOYET | She says you have it, and you may be gone. |
| [[King|KING]] | Say to her we have measured many miles<br>To tread a measure with her on this grass. |
| BOYET | They say that they have measured many a mile<br>To tread a measure with you on this grass. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | It is not so. Ask them how many inches<br>Is in one mile. If they have measured many,<br>The measure then of one is easily told. |
| BOYET | If to come hither you have measured miles,<br>And many miles, the Princess bids you tell<br>How many inches doth fill up one mile. |
| BIRON | Tell her we measure them by weary steps. |
| BOYET | She hears herself. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | How many weary steps<br>Of many weary miles you have o’ergone<br>Are numbered in the travel of one mile? |
| BIRON | We number nothing that we spend for you.<br>Our duty is so rich, so infinite,<br>That we may do it still without account.<br>Vouchsafe to show the sunshine of your face<br>That we, like savages, may worship it. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | My face is but a [[Moon|moon]], and clouded, too. |
| [[King|KING]] | Blessed are clouds to do as such clouds do.<br>Vouchsafe, bright [[Moon|moon]], and these thy [[Stars|stars]], to shine,<br>Those clouds removed, upon our watery eyne. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | O vain petitioner, beg a greater matter.<br>Thou now requests but moonshine in the [[Water|water]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | Then in our measure do but vouchsafe one change.<br>Thou bid’st me beg; this begging is not strange. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Play, music, then. |
| *(didascalia)* |  Music plays |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Nay, you must do it soon.<br>Not yet?—no [[Dance|dance]]! Thus change I like the [[Moon|moon]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | Will you not [[Dance|dance]]? How come you thus estranged? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | You took the [[Moon|moon]] at full, but now she’s changed. |
| [[King|KING]] | Yet still she is the [[Moon|moon]], and I the man. |
| *(didascalia)* |   215 |
| [[King|KING]] | The music plays, vouchsafe some motion to it. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Our ears vouchsafe it. |
| [[King|KING]] | But your legs should do it. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Since you are strangers and come here by chance<br>We’ll not be nice. Take hands. We will not [[Dance|dance]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | Why take we hands, then? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Only to part friends.<br>Curtsy, sweethearts, and so the measure ends. |
| [[King|KING]] | More measure of this measure, be not nice. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | We can afford no more at such a price. |
| [[King|KING]] | Price you yourselves. What buys your company? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Your absence only. |
| [[King|KING]] | That can never be. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Then cannot we be bought, and so adieu—<br>Twice to your visor, and half once to you. |
| [[King|KING]] | If you deny to [[Dance|dance]], let’s hold more chat. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | In private, then. |
| [[King|KING]] | I am best pleased with that.<br>The [[King]] and [[Rosaline]] talk apart |
| BIRON | (to the Princess, taking her for [[Rosaline]])<br>White-handed mistress, one sweet word with thee. |
| PRINCESS | Honey and milk and sugar—there is three. |
| BIRON | Nay then, two treys, an if you grow so nice—<br>Metheglin, wort, and malmsey—[[Well|well]] run, dice!<br>There’s half-a-dozen sweets. |
| PRINCESS | Seventh sweet, adieu.<br>Since you can cog, I’ll play no more with you. |
| BIRON | One word in secret. |
| PRINCESS | Let it not be sweet. |
| BIRON | Thou griev’st my gall. |
| PRINCESS | Gall—bitter! |
| BIRON | Therefore meet.<br>Biron and the Princess talk apart |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | (to [[Maria]], taking her for Catherine)<br>Will you vouchsafe with me to change a word? |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | Name it. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Fair lady— |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | Say you so? Fair lord-<br>Take that for your ‘fair lady’. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Please it you,<br>As much in private, and I’ll bid adieu.<br>[[Dumaine]] and [[Maria]] talk apart |
| CATHERINE | What, was your visor made without a tongue? |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | (taking Catherine for [[Maria]])<br>I know the reason, lady, why you ask. |
| CATHERINE | O, for your reason ! Quickly, sir, I long. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | You have a [[Double|double]] tongue within your mask,<br>And would afford my speechless visor half. |
| CATHERINE | ‘Veal’, quoth the Dutchman. Is not veal a calf? |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | A calf, fair lady? |
| CATHERINE | No, a fair lord calf. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | Let’s part the word. |
| CATHERINE | No, I’ll not be your half.<br>Take all and wean it, it may prove an ox. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | Look how you butt yourself in these sharp mocks !<br>Will you give horns, chaste lady? Do not so. |
| CATHERINE | Then die a calf before your horns do grow. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | One word in private with you ere I die. |
| CATHERINE | Bleat softly, then. The butcher hears you cry.<br>[[Longueville]] and Catherine talk apart |
| BOYET | The tongues of mocking wenches are as keen<br>As is the razor’s edge invisible,<br>Cutting a smaller hair than may be seen,<br>Above the sense of sense; so sensible<br>Seemeth their conference. Their conceits have wings<br>Fleeter than arrows, bullets, [[Wind|wind]], thought, swifter things. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Not one word more, my maids. Break off, break off. |
| BIRON | By heaven, all dry-beaten with pure scoff! |
| [[King|KING]] | Farewell, mad wenches, you have simple wits. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the [[King]], lords, and blackamoors |
| *(didascalia)* | The ladies unmask |
| PRINCESS | Twenty adieus, my frozen Muscovites.<br>Are these the breed of wits so wondered at? |
| BOYET | Tapers they are, with your sweet breaths puffed out. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | [[Well]]-liking wits they have; gross, gross; fat, fat. |
| PRINCESS | O [[Poverty|poverty]] in wit, kingly-poor flout !<br>Will they not, think you, hang themselves tonight,<br>Or ever but in visors show their faces?<br>This pert Biron was out of count’nance quite. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Ah, they were all in lamentable cases.<br>The [[King]] was weeping-ripe for a good word. |
| PRINCESS | Biron did swear himself out of all suit. |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | [[Dumaine]] was at my service, and his [[Sword|sword]].<br>‘Non point,’ quoth I. My servant straight was mute. |
| CATHERINE | Lord [[Longueville]] said I came o’er his heart,<br>And trow you what he called me? |
| PRINCESS | ‘Qualm’, perhaps. |
| CATHERINE | Yes, in good [[Faith|faith]]. |
| PRINCESS | Go, sickness as thou art. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | [[Well]], better wits have worn plain statute-caps.<br>But will you hear? The [[King]] is my [[Love|love]] sworn. |
| PRINCESS | And quick Biron hath plighted [[Faith|faith]] to me. |
| CATHERINE | And [[Longueville]] was for my service born. |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | [[Dumaine]] is mine, as sure as bark on tree. |
| BOYET | Madam, and pretty mistresses, give ear.<br>Immediately they will again be here<br>In their own shapes, for it can never be<br>They will digest this harsh indignity. |
| PRINCESS | Will they return? |
| BOYET | They will, they will, God knows,<br>And leap for joy, though they are lame with blows.<br>Therefore change favours, and when they repair,<br>Blow like sweet roses in this [[Summer|summer]] air. |
| PRINCESS | How ‘blow’ ? How ‘blow’ ? Speak to be understood. |
| BOYET | Fair ladies masked are roses in their bud;<br>Dismasked, their damask sweet commixture shown,<br>Are angels vailing clouds, or roses blown. |
| PRINCESS | Avaunt, perplexity! What shall we do<br>If they return in their own shapes to woo? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Good madam, if by me you’ll be advised,<br>Let’s mock them still, as [[Well|well]] known as disguised.<br>Let us complain to them what fools were here,<br>Disguised like Muscovites in shapeless gear,<br>And [[Wonder|wonder]] what they were, and to what end<br>Their shallow shows, and prologue vilely penned,<br>And their rough carriage so ridiculous,<br>Should be presented at our tent to us. |
| BOYET | Ladies, withdraw. The gallants are at hand. |
| PRINCESS | Whip, to our tents, as roes run over land! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the ladies |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[King]], Biron, [[Dumaine]], and [[Longueville]], as themselves |
| [[King|KING]] | Fair sir, God save you. Where’s the Princess? |
| BOYET | Gone to her tent. Please it your majesty<br>Command me any service to her thither? |
| [[King|KING]] | That she vouchsafe me audience for one word. |
| BOYET | I will, and so will she, I know, my lord. Exit |
| BIRON | This fellow pecks up wit as pigeons peas,<br>And utters it again when God doth please.<br>He is wit’s pedlar, and retails his wares<br>At wakes and wassails, meetings, markets, fairs.<br>And we that sell by gross, the Lord doth know,<br>Have not the grace to grace it with such show.<br>This gallant pins the wenches on his sleeve.<br>Had he been Adam, he had tempted Eve.<br>A can carve too, and lisp, why, this is he<br>That kissed his hand away in courtesy.<br>This is the ape of form, Monsieur the Nice,<br>That when he plays at tables chides the dice<br>In honourable terms. Nay, he can sing<br>A mean most meanly, and in ushering<br>Mend him who can. The ladies call him sweet.<br>The stairs as he treads on them kiss his feet.<br>This is the flower that smiles on everyone<br>To show his teeth as white as whales bone,<br>And consciences that will not die in debt<br>Pay him the due of ‘honey-tongued’ Boyet. |
| [[King|KING]] | A blister on his sweet tongue with my heart,<br>That put Armado’s page out of his part! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the ladies and Boyet |
| BIRON | See where it comes. Behaviour, what wert thou<br>Till this madman showed thee, and what art thou<br>now ? |
| [[King|KING]] | All hail, sweet madam, and fair [[Time|time]] of day! |
| PRINCESS | ‘Fair’ in ‘all hail’ is foul, as I conceive. |
| [[King|KING]] | Construe my speeches better, if you may. |
| PRINCESS | Then wish me better. I will give you leave. |
| [[King|KING]] | We came to visit you, and purpose now<br>To lead you to our [[Court|court]]. Vouchsafe it, then. |
| PRINCESS | This field shall hold me, and so hold your vow.<br>Nor God nor I delights in perjured men. |
| [[King|KING]] | Rebuke me not for that which you provoke.<br>The virtue of your eye must break my oath. |
| PRINCESS | You nickname virtue. ‘Vice’ you should have spoke,<br>For virtue’s office never breaks men’s troth.<br>Now by my maiden [[Honour|honour]], yet as pure<br>As the unsullied lily, I protest,<br>A world of torments though I should endure,<br>I would not yield to be your [[house]]’s guest,<br>So much I hate a breaking cause to be<br>Of heavenly oaths, vowed with integrity. |
| [[King|KING]] | O, you have lived in desolation here,<br>Unseen, unvisited, much to our shame. |
| PRINCESS | Not so, my lord. It is not so, I swear.<br>We have had pastimes here, and pleasant game.<br>A mess of Russians left us but of late. |
| [[King|KING]] | How, madam? Russians? |
| PRINCESS | Ay, in [[Truth|truth]], my lord.<br>Trim gallants, full of courtship and of state. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Madam, speak true.—It is not so, my lord.<br>My lady, to the manner of the days,<br>In courtesy gives undeserving praise.<br>We four indeed confronted were with four<br>In Russian habit. Here they stayed an hour,<br>And talked apace, and in that hour, my lord,<br>They did not bless us with one happy word.<br>I dare not call them fools, but this I think:<br>When they are thirsty, fools would fain have drink. |
| BIRON | This jest is dry to me. Gentle sweet,<br>Your wits makes wise things foolish. When we greet,<br>With eyes’ best seeing, heaven’s fiery eye,<br>By light we lose light. Your capacity<br>Is of that [[Nature|nature]] that to your huge store<br>Wise things seem foolish, and rich things but poor. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | This proves you wise and rich, for in my eye— |
| BIRON | I am a [[Fool|fool]], and full of [[Poverty|poverty]]. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | But that you take what doth to you belong<br>It were a fault to snatch words from my tongue. |
| BIRON | O, I am yours, and all that I possess. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | All the [[Fool|fool]] mine! |
| BIRON | I cannot give you less. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Which of the visors was it that you wore? |
| BIRON | Where? When? What visor? Why demand you this? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | There, then, that visor, that superfluous case,<br>That hid the worse and showed the better face. |
| [[King|KING]] | (aside to the lords)<br>We were descried. They’ll mock us now, downright. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | (aside to the [[King]])<br>Let us confess, and turn it to a jest. |
| PRINCESS | Amazed, my lord? Why looks your highness sad? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Help, hold his brows, he’ll swoon. Why look you<br>pale?<br>Seasick, I think, coming from Muscovy. |
| BIRON | Thus pour the [[Stars|stars]] down plagues for perjury.<br>Can any face of brass hold longer out?<br>Here stand I, lady. Dart thy skill at me—<br>Bruise me with scorn, confound me with a flout,<br>Thrust thy sharp wit quite through my ignorance,<br>Cut me to pieces with thy keen conceit,<br>And I will wish thee nevermore to [[Dance|dance]],<br>Nor nevermore in Russian habit wait.<br>O, never will I trust to speeches penned,<br>Nor to the motion of a schoolboy’s tongue,<br>Nor never come in visor to my friend,<br>Nor woo in rhyme, like a blind harper’s [[Song|song]].<br>Taffeta phrases, silken terms precise,<br>Three-piled hyperboles, spruce affectation,<br>Figures pedantical-these [[Summer|summer]] flies<br>Have blown me full of maggot ostentation.<br>I do forswear them, and I here protest,<br>By this white glove—how white the hand, God<br>knows!—<br>Henceforth my wooing mind shall be expressed<br>In russet yeas, and honest kersey noes.<br>And to begin, wench, so God help me, law!<br>My [[Love|love]] to thee is sound, sans crack or flaw. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Sans ‘sans’, I pray you. |
| BIRON | Yet I have a trick<br>Of the old rage. Bear with me, I am sick.<br>I’ll leave it by degrees. Soft, let us see.<br>Write ‘Lord have [[Mercy|mercy]] on us’ on those three.<br>They are infected, in their hearts it lies.<br>They have the plague, and caught it of your eyes.<br>These lords are visited, you are not free;<br>For the Lord’s tokens on you do I see. |
| PRINCESS | No, they are free that gave these tokens to us. |
| BIRON | Our states are forfeit. Seek not to undo us. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | It is not so, for how can this be true,<br>That you stand forfeit, being those that sue? |
| BIRON | Peace, for I will not have to do with you. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Nor shall not, if I do as I intend. |
| BIRON | (to the lords)<br>Speak for yourselves. My wit is at an end. |
| [[King|KING]] | Teach us, sweet madam, for our rude transgression<br>Some fair excuse. |
| PRINCESS | The fairest is confession.<br>Were not you here but even now disguised? |
| [[King|KING]] | Madam, I was. |
| PRINCESS | And were you [[Well|well]] advised? |
| [[King|KING]] | I was, fair madam. |
| PRINCESS | When you then were here,<br>What did you whisper in your lady’s ear? |
| [[King|KING]] | That more than all the world I did respect her. |
| PRINCESS | When she shall challenge this, you will reject her. |
| [[King|KING]] | Upon mine [[Honour|honour]], no. |
| PRINCESS | Peace, peace,forbear.<br>Your oath once broke, you force not to forswear. |
| [[King|KING]] | Despise me when I break this oath of mine. |
| PRINCESS | I will, and therefore keep it. [[Rosaline]],<br>What did the Russian whisper in your ear? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Madam, he swore that he did hold me dear<br>As precious eyesight, and did value me<br>Above this world, adding thereto moreover<br>That he would wed me, or else die my [[Lover|lover]]. |
| PRINCESS | God give thee joy of him! The noble lord<br>Most honourably doth uphold his word. |
| [[King|KING]] | What mean you, madam? By my life, my troth,<br>I never swore this lady such an oath. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | By heaven, you did, and to confirm it plain,<br>You gave me this. But take it, sir, again. |
| [[King|KING]] | My [[Faith|faith]] and this the Princess I did give.<br>I knew her by this [[Jewel|jewel]] on her sleeve. |
| PRINCESS | Pardon me, sir, this [[Jewel|jewel]] did she wear,<br>And Lord Biron, I thank him, is my dear.<br>(To Biron) What, will you have me, or your pearl again? |
| BIRON | Neither of either. I remit both twain.<br>I see the trick on’t. Here was a consent,<br>Knowing aforehand of our merriment,<br>To dash it like a Christmas [[Comedy|comedy]].<br>Some carry-tale, some please-man, some slight zany,<br>Some mumble-news, some trencher-knight, some Dick<br>That smiles his cheek in years, and knows the trick<br>To make my lady laugh when she’s disposed,<br>Told our intents before, which once disclosed,<br>The ladies did change favours, and then we,<br>Following the signs, wooed but the sign of she.<br>Now, to our perjury to add more terror,<br>We are again forsworn, in will and error.<br>Much upon this ’tis, (to Boyet) and might not you<br>Forestall our sport, to make us thus untrue?<br>Do not you know my lady’s foot by th’ square,<br>And laugh upon the apple of her eye,<br>And stand between her back, sir, and the [[Fire|fire]],<br>Holding a trencher, jesting merrily?<br>You put our page out. Go, you are allowed.<br>Die when you will, a smock shall be your shroud.<br>You leer upon me, do you? There’s an eye<br>Wounds like a leaden [[Sword|sword]]. |
| BOYET | Full merrily<br>Hath this brave manège, this career been run. |
| BIRON | Lo, he is tilting straight. Peace, I have done. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Costard]] the clown |
| BIRON | Welcome, pure wit. Thou partest a fair fray. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | O Lord, sir, they would know 485<br>Whether the three Worthies shall come in or no. |
| BIRON | What, are there but three? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | No, sir, but it is vara fine,<br>For everyone pursents three. |
| BIRON | And three times thrice is nine. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Not so, sir, under correction, sir, I [[Hope|hope]] it is not so.<br>You cannot beg us, sir. I can assure you, sir, we<br>know what we know.<br>I [[Hope|hope]], sir, three times thrice, sir— |
| BIRON | Is not nine? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Under correction, sir, we know whereuntil it doth amount. |
| BIRON | By Jove, I always took three threes for nine. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | O Lord, sir, it were pity you should get your living by reck’ning, sir. BIRON How much is it? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | O Lord, sir, the parties themselves, the actors, sir, will show whereuntil it doth amount. For mine own part, I am, as they say, but to parfect one man in one poor man, Pompion the Great, sir. |
| BIRON | Art thou one of the Worthies? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | It pleased them to think me worthy of [[Pompey]] the Great. For mine own part, I know not the degree of the Worthy, but I am to stand for him. |
| BIRON | Go, bid them prepare. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | We will turn it finely off, sir. We will take some care. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[King|KING]] | Biron, they will shame us. Let them not approach. |
| BIRON | We are shame-proof, my lord, and ‘tis some policy<br>To have one show worse than the [[King]]’s and his<br>company. |
| [[King|KING]] | I say they shall not come. |
| PRINCESS | Nay, my good lord, let me o’errule you now.<br>That sport best pleases that doth least know how.<br>Where zeal strives to content, and the contents |
| *(didascalia)* | Dies in the zeal of that which it presents, |
| PRINCESS | There form confounded makes most form in mirth,<br>When great things labouring perish in their birth. |
| BIRON | A right description of our sport, my lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado the braggart |
| ARMADO | (to the [[King]]) Anointed, I implore so much expense of thy royal sweet breath as will utter a brace of words. |
| *(didascalia)* | Armado and the [[King]] speak apart |
| PRINCESS | Doth this man serve God? |
| BIRON | Why ask you? |
| PRINCESS | A speaks not like a man of God his making. |
| ARMADO | That is all one, my fair sweet honey monarch, for, I protest, the schoolmaster is exceeding fantastical, too-too vain, too-too vain. But we will put it, as they say, to fortuna de la guerra. I wish you the peace of mind, most royal couplement. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[King|KING]] | Here is like to be a good presence of Worthies. He presents [[Hector]] of Troy, the swain [[Pompey]] the Great, the parish curate Alexander, Armado’s page Hercules, the pedant Judas Maccabeus, And if these four Worthies in their first show thrive, These four will change habits and present the other five. |
| BIRON | There is five in the first show. |
| [[King|KING]] | You are deceived, ’tis not so. |
| BIRON | The pedant, the braggart, the hedge-priest, the [[Fool|fool]],<br>and the boy,<br>Abate throw at novum and the whole world again<br>Cannot pick out five such, take each one in his vein. |
| [[King|KING]] | The [[Ship|ship]] is under sail, and here she comes amain. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Costard]] the clown as [[Pompey]] |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | (as [[Pompey]])<br>I [[Pompey]] am— |
| BIRON | You lie, you are not he. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | (as [[Pompey]])<br>I [[Pompey]] am— |
| BOYET | With leopard’s head on knee. |
| BIRON | [[Well]] said, old mocker. I must needs be friends with thee. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | (as [[Pompey]])<br>I [[Pompey]] am, [[Pompey]] surnamed the Big. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | ‘The Great’. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | It is ‘Great’, sir—<br>(As [[Pompey]]) [[Pompey]] surnamed the Great,<br>That oft in field with targe and shield did make my<br>foe to sweat,<br>And travelling along this coast I here am come by<br>chance,<br>And lay my arms before the legs of this sweet lass of<br>France.—<br>If your ladyship would say ‘Thanks, [[Pompey]]’, I had<br>done. |
| *(didascalia)* | PRTNCESS Great thanks, great [[Pompey]]. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | ‘Tis not so much worth, but I [[Hope|hope]] I was perfect.<br>I made a little fault in ‘great’. |
| BIRON | My hat to a halfpenny [[Pompey]] proves the best<br>Worthy.<br>[[Costard]] stands aside. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Nathaniel the curate as Alexander |
| NATHANIEL | (as Alexander)<br>When in the world I lived I was the world’s commander.<br>By east, west, north, and south, I spread my conquering might.<br>My scutcheon plain declares that I am Alisander. |
| BOYET | Your nose says no, you are not, for it stands too right. |
| BIRON | (to Boyet)<br>Your nose smells ‘no’ in this, most tender-smelling knight. |
| PRINCESS | The conqueror is dismayed. Proceed, good Alexander. |
| NATHANIEL | (as Alexander)<br>When in the world I lived I was the world’s commander. |
| BOYET | Most true, ’tis right, you were so, Alisander. |
| BIRON | (to [[Costard]]) [[Pompey]] the Great. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Your servant, and [[Costard]]. |
| BIRON | Take away the conqueror, take away Alisander. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | (to Nathaniel) O, sir, you have overthrown Alisander the Conqueror. You will be scraped out of the painted cloth for this. Your [[Lion|lion]] that holds his poleaxe sitting on a close-stool will be given to [[Ajax]]. He will be the ninth Worthy. A conqueror and afeard to speak? Run away for shame, Alisander. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Nathaniel the curate |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | There, an’t shall please you, a foolish mild man, an honest man, look you, and soon dashed. He is a marvellous good neighbour, [[Faith|faith]], and a very good bowler, but for Alisander—alas, you see how ‘tis—a little o’erparted. But there are Worthies a-coming will speak their mind in some other sort. |
| PRINCESS | Stand aside, good [[Pompey]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Holofernes the pedant as Judas, and the boy Mote as Hercules |
| HOLOFERNES | Great Hercules is presented by this imp,<br>Whose club killed Cerberus, that three-headed<br>canus,<br><br>And when he was a babe, a [[Child|child]], a shrimp,<br>Thus did he strangle serpents in his manus.<br><br>Quoniam he seemeth in minority,<br>Ergo I come with this apology.<br>(To Mote) Keep some state in thy exit, and vanish. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Mote |
| HOLOFERNES | (as Judas)<br>Judas I am— |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | A Judas? |
| HOLOFERNES | Not Iscariot, sir.<br>(As Judas) Judas I am, yclept Maccabeus. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Judas Maccabeus clipped is plain Judas. |
| BIRON | A kissing traitor. How art thou proved Judas ? |
| HOLOFERNES | (as Judas)<br>Judas I am— |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | The more shame for you, Judas. |
| HOLOFERNES | What mean you, sir? |
| BOYET | To make Judas hang himself. |
| HOLOFERNES | Begin, sir. You are my elder. |
| BIRON | [[Well]] followed—Judas was hanged on an elder. |
| HOLOFERNES | I will not be put out of countenance. 601 |
| BIRON | Because thou hast no face. |
| HOLOFERNES | What is this? |
| BOYET | A cittern-head. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | The head of a bodkin. |
| BIRON | A [[Death|death]]’s face in a ring. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | The face of an old Roman coin, scarce seen. |
| BOYET | The pommel of Caesar’s falchion. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | The carved-bone face on a flask. |
| BIRON | Saint George’s half-cheek in a brooch. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Ay, and in a brooch of lead. |
| BIRON | Ay, and worn in the cap of a tooth-drawer. And now forward, for we have put thee in countenance. |
| HOLOFERNES | You have put me out of countenance. |
| BIRON | False, we have given thee faces. |
| HOLOFERNES | But you have outfaced them all. |
| BIRON | An thou wert a [[Lion|lion]], we would do so. |
| BOYET | Therefore, as he is an ass, let him go.<br>And so adieu, sweet Jude. Nay, why dost thou stay? |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | For the latter end of his name. |
| BIRON | For the ass to the Jude. Give it him. Jud-as, away. |
| HOLOFERNES | This is not generous, not gentle, not humble. |
| BOYET | A light for Monsieur Judas. It grows dark, he may stumble. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Holofernes |
| PRINCESS | Alas, poor Maccabeus, how hath he been baited ! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado the braggart as [[Hector]] |
| BIRON | Hide thy head, [[Achilles]], here comes [[Hector]] in arms. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Though my mocks come [[Home|home]] by me, I will now be merry. |
| [[King|KING]] | [[Hector]] was but a Trojan in respect of this. |
| BOYET | But is this [[Hector]]? |
| [[King|KING]] | I think [[Hector]] was not so clean-timbered. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | His leg is too big for [[Hector]]’s. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | More calf, certain. |
| BOYET | No, he is best endowed in the small. |
| BIRON | This cannot be [[Hector]]. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | He’s a god, or a painter, for he makes faces. |
| ARMADO | (as [[Hector]])<br>The armipotent Mars, of lances the almighty,<br>Gave [[Hector]] a gift— |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | A gilt nutmeg. |
| BIRON | A lemon. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | Stuck with cloves. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | NO, cloven. |
| ARMADO | Peace I<br>(As [[Hector]]) The armipotent Mars, of lances the<br>almighty,<br>Gave [[Hector]] a gift, the heir of Ilion,<br>A man so breathed that certain he would fight, yea,<br>From morn till [[Night|night]], out of his pavilion.<br>I am that flower— |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | That mint. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | That colombine. |
| ARMADO | Sweet Lord [[Longueville]], rein thy tongue. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | I must rather give it the rein, for it runs against [[Hector]]. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Ay, and [[Hector]]’s a greyhound. |
| ARMADO | The sweet war-man is dead and rotten. Sweet chucks, beat not the bones of the buried. When he breathed he was a man. But I will forward with my device. (To the Princess) Sweet royalty, bestow on me the sense of hearing.<br>Biron steps forth |
| PRINCESS | Speak, brave [[Hector]], we are much delighted. |
| ARMADO | I do adore thy sweet grace’s slipper. |
| BOYET | Loves her by the foot. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | He may not by the yard. |
| ARMADO | (as [[Hector]])<br>This [[Hector]] far surmounted Hannibal. |
| *(didascalia)* |  |
| ARMADO | The party is gone. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Fellow [[Hector]], she is gone, she is two months on her way. |
| ARMADO | What meanest thou? |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | [[Faith]], unless you play the honest Trojan the poor wench is cast away. She’s quick. The [[Child|child]] brags in her belly already. ’Tis yours. |
| ARMADO | Dost thou infamonize me among potentates? Thou shalt die. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | Then shall [[Hector]] be whipped for Jaquenetta that is quick by him, and hanged for [[Pompey]] that is dead by him. 675 |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Most rare [[Pompey]]! |
| BOYET | Renowned [[Pompey]]! |
| BIRON | Greater than great—great, great, great [[Pompey]], [[Pompey]] the Huge. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | [[Hector]] trembles. |
| BIRON | [[Pompey]] is moved. More Ates, more Ates—stir them on, stir them on! |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | [[Hector]] will challenge him. |
| BIRON | Ay, if a have no more man’s [[Blood|blood]] in his belly than will sup a flea. |
| ARMADO | By the North Pole, I do challenge thee. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | I will not fight with a pole, like a northern man. I’ll slash, I’ll do it by the [[Sword|sword]]. I bepray you, let me borrow my arms again. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Room for the incensed Worthies. |
| [[Costard|COSTARD]] | I’ll do it in my shirt. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Most resolute [[Pompey]]. |
| MOTE | (aside to Armado) Master, let me take you a buttonhole lower. Do you not see [[Pompey]] is uncasing for the combat? What mean you? You will lose your reputation. |
| ARMADO | Gentlemen and soldiers, pardon me. I will not combat in my shirt. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | You may not deny it, [[Pompey]] hath made the challenge. |
| ARMADO | Sweet bloods, I both may and will. |
| BIRON | What reason have you for’t? |
| ARMADO | The naked [[Truth|truth]] of it is, I have no shirt. I go woolward for penance. |
| *(didascalia)* | MOTE True, and it was enjoined him in Rome for want of linen, since when I’ll be sworn he wore none but a dish-clout of Jaquenetta’s, and that a wears next his heart, for a favour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a messenger, Monsieur Mercadé |
| ARMADO | MERCADÉ<br>God save you, madam. |
| PRINCESS | Welcome, Mercadé,<br>But that thou interrupt’st our merriment.<br>MERCADÉ<br>I am sorry, madam, for the news I bring<br>Is heavy in my tongue. The [[King]] your father— |
| PRINCESS | Dead, for my life.<br>MERCADÉ Even so. My tale is told. |
| BIRON | Worthies, away. The scene begins to cloud. |
| ARMADO | For mine own part, I breathe free breath. I have seen the day of wrong through the little hole of discretion, and I will right myself like a soldier. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the Worthies |
| [[King|KING]] | How fares your majesty ? |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Boyet, prepare. I will away tonight. |
| [[King|KING]] | Madam, not so, I do beseech you stay. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Prepare, I say. I thank you, gracious lords,<br>For all your fair endeavours, and entreat,<br>Out of a new-sad [[Soul|soul]], that you vouchsafe<br>In your rich wisdom to excuse or hide<br>The liberal opposition of our spirits.<br>If overboldly we have borne ourselves<br>In the converse of breath, your gentleness<br>Was guilty of it. Farewell, worthy lord.<br>A heavy heart bears not a nimble tongue.<br>Excuse me so coming too short of thanks,<br>For my great suit so easily obtained. |
| [[King|KING]] | The extreme parts of [[Time|time]] extremely forms<br>All causes to the purpose of his [[Speed|speed]],<br>And often at his very loose decides<br>That which long process could not arbitrate.<br>And though the mourning brow of progeny<br>Forbid the smiling courtesy of [[Love|love]]<br>The holy suit which fain it would convince,<br>Yet since [[Love|love]]’s argument was first on foot,<br>Let not the cloud of sorrow jostle it<br>From what it purposed, since to wail friends lost<br>Is not by much so wholesome-profitable<br>As to rejoice at friends but newly found. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | I understand you not. My griefs are [[Double|double]]. |
| BIRON | Honest plain words best pierce the ear of grief,<br>And by these badges understand the [[King]].<br>For your fair sakes have we neglected [[Time|time]],<br>Played foul play with our oaths. Your [[Beauty|beauty]], ladies,<br>Hath much deformed us, fashioning our humours<br>Even to the opposed end of our intents,<br>And what in us hath seemed ridiculous—<br>As [[Love|love]] is full of unbefitting strains,<br>All wanton as a [[Child|child]], skipping and vain,<br>Formed by the eye and therefore like the eye,<br>Full of strange shapes, of habits and of forms,<br>Varying in subjects as the eye doth roll<br>To every varied object in his glance;<br>Which parti-coated presence of loose [[Love|love]]<br>Put on by us, if in your heavenly eyes<br>Have misbecomed our oaths and gravities,<br>Those heavenly eyes that look into these faults<br>Suggested us to make them. Therefore, ladies,<br>Our [[Love|love]] being yours, the error that [[Love|love]] makes<br>Is likewise yours. We to ourselves prove false<br>By being once false for ever to be true<br>To those that make us both—fair ladies, you.<br>And even that falsehood, in itself a sin,<br>Thus purifies itself and turns to grace. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | We have received your letters full of [[Love|love]],<br>Your favours the ambassadors of [[Love|love]],<br>And in our maiden council rated them<br>At courtship, pleasant jest, and courtesy,<br>As bombast and as lining to the [[Time|time]].<br>But more devout than this in our respects<br>Have we not been, and therefore met your loves<br>In their own fashion, like a merriment. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | Our letters, madam, showed much more than jest. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | So did our looks. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | We did not quote them so. |
| [[King|KING]] | Now, at the latest minute of the hour,<br>Grant us your loves. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | A [[Time|time]], methinks, too short<br>To make a world-without-end bargain in.<br>No, no, my lord, your grace is perjured much,<br>Full of dear guiltiness, and therefore this:<br>If for my [[Love|love]]—as there is no such cause—<br>You will do aught, this shall you do for me:<br>Your oath I will not trust, but go with [[Speed|speed]]<br>To some forlorn and naked hermitage<br>Remote from all the pleasures of the world.<br>There stay until the twelve celestial signs<br>Have brought about the annual reckoning.<br>If this austere, insociable life<br>Change not your offer made in heat of [[Blood|blood]];<br>If frosts and fasts, hard lodging and thin weeds<br>Nip not the gaudy blossoms of your [[Love|love]],<br>But that it bear this trial and last [[Love|love]],<br>Then at the expiration of the year<br>Come challenge me, challenge me by these deserts,<br>And, by this virgin palm now kissing thine,<br>I will be thine, and till that instance shut<br>My woeful [[Self|self]] up in a mourning [[house]],<br>Raining the [[Tears|tears]] of lamentation<br>For the remembrance of my father’s [[Death|death]].<br>If this thou do deny, let our hands part,<br>Neither entitled in the other’s heart. |
| [[King|KING]] | If this, or more than this, I would deny,<br>To flatter up these powers of mine with rest<br>The sudden hand of [[Death|death]] close up mine eye.<br>Hence, hermit, then. My heart is in thy breast.<br>They talk apart |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | (to Catherine)<br>But what to me, my [[Love|love]]? But what to me?<br>A wife? |
| CATHERINE | A beard, fair health, and honesty.<br>With three-fold [[Love|love]] I wish you all these three. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | O, shall I say ‘I thank you, gentle wife’? |
| CATHERINE | Not so, my lord. A twelvemonth and a day<br>I’ll mark no words that smooth-faced wooers say.<br>Come when the [[King]] doth to my lady come;<br>Then if I have much [[Love|love]], I’ll give you some. |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | I’ll serve thee true and faithfully till then. |
| CATHERINE | Yet swear not, lest ye be forsworn again.<br>They talk apart |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | What says [[Maria]]? |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | At the twelvemonth’s end<br>I’ll change my black gown for a faithful friend. |
| [[Longueville|LONGUEVILLE]] | I’ll stay with patience; but the [[Time|time]] is long. |
| [[Maria|MARIA]] | The liker you—few taller are so young.<br>They talk apart |
| BIRON | (to [[Rosaline]])<br>Studies my lady? Mistress, look on me.<br>Behold the [[Window|window]] of my heart, mine eye,<br>What humble suit attends thy answer there.<br>Impose some service on me for thy [[Love|love]]. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Oft have I heard of you, my lord Biron,<br>Before I saw you; and the world’s large tongue<br>Proclaims you for a man replete with mocks,<br>Full of comparisons and wounding flouts,<br>Which you on all estates will execute<br>That lie within the [[Mercy|mercy]] of your wit.<br>To weed this wormwood from your fruitful brain,<br>And therewithal to win me if you please,<br>Without the which I am not to be won,<br>You shall this twelvemonth term from day to day<br>Visit the speechless sick and still converse<br>With groaning wretches, and your task shall be<br>With all the fierce endeavour of your wit<br>To enforce the pained impotent to smile. |
| BIRON | To move wild laughter in the throat of [[Death|death]]?—<br>It cannot be, it is impossible.<br>Mirth cannot move a [[Soul|soul]] in agony. |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | Why, that’s the way to choke a gibing spirit,<br>Whose influence is begot of that loose grace<br>Which shallow laughing hearers give to fools.<br>A jest’s prosperity lies in the ear<br>Of him that hears it, never in the tongue<br>Of him that makes it. Then if sickly ears,<br>Deafed with the clamours of their own dear groans,<br>Will hear your idle scorns, continue then,<br>And I will have you and that fault withal.<br>But if they will not, throw away that spirit,<br>And I shall find you empty of that fault,<br>Right joyful of your [[Reformation|reformation]]. |
| BIRON | A twelvemonth? [[Well]], befall what will befall,<br>I’ll jest a twelvemonth in an hospital. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | (to the [[King]])<br>Ay, sweet my lord, and so I take my leave. |
| [[King|KING]] | No, madam, we will bring you on your way. |
| BIRON | Our wooing doth not end like an old play.<br>Jack hath not Jill. These ladies’ courtesy<br>Might [[Well|well]] have made our sport a [[Comedy|comedy]]. |
| [[King|KING]] | Come, sir, it wants a twelvemonth an’ a day,<br>And then ’twill end. |
| BIRON | That’s too long for a play. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado the braggart |
| ARMADO | (to the [[King]]) Sweet majesty, vouchsafe me. |
| [[Queen|QUEEN]] | Was not that [[Hector]]? |
| [[Dumaine|DUMAINE]] | The worthy knight of Troy. |
| ARMADO | I will kiss thy royal finger and take leave.<br>I am a votary, I have vowed to Jaquenetta<br>To hold the plough for her sweet [[Love|love]] three year.<br>But, most esteemed [[Greatness|greatness]], will you hear the<br>dialogue that the two learned men have compiled in<br>praise of the owl and the cuckoo ? It should have<br>followed in the end of our show. |
| [[King|KING]] | Call them forth quickly, we will do so. |
| ARMADO | Holla, approach! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Holofernes, Nathaniel, [[Costard]], Mote, Dull, Jaquenetta, and others |
| ARMADO | This side is Hiems, [[Winter|winter]],<br>This Ver, the [[Spring|spring]], the one maintained by the owl,<br>The other by the cuckoo. Ver, begin. |
| [[Spring|SPRING]] | (sings)<br>When daisies pied and violets blue,<br>And lady-smocks, all silver-white,<br>And cuckoo-buds of yellow hue<br>Do paint the meadows with delight,<br>The cuckoo then on every tree<br>Mocks married men, for thus sings he:<br>Cuckoo!<br>Cuckoo, cuckoo—O word of [[Fear|fear]],<br>Unpleasing to a married ear.<br>When shepherds pipe on oaten straws,<br>And merry larks are ploughmen’s clocks;<br>When turtles tread, and rooks and daws,<br>And maidens bleach their [[Summer|summer]] smocks,<br>The cuckoo then on every tree<br>Mocks married men, for thus sings he:<br>Cuckoo!<br>Cuckoo, cuckoo—O word of [[Fear|fear]],<br>Unpleasing to a married ear. |
| [[Winter|WINTER]] | (sings)<br>When icicles hang by the wall,<br>And Dick the shepherd blows his nail,<br>And Tom bears logs into the hall,<br>And milk comes frozen [[Home|home]] in pail;<br>When [[Blood|blood]] is nipped, and ways be foul,<br>Then nightly sings the staring owl:<br>Tu-whit, tu-whoo!—a merry note,<br>While greasy Joan doth keel the pot.<br>When all aloud the [[Wind|wind]] doth blow,<br>And coughing drowns the parson’s saw,<br>And [[Birds|birds]] sit brooding in the [[Snow|snow]],<br>And Marian’s nose looks red and raw;<br>When roasted crabs hiss in the bowl,<br>Then nightly sings the staring owl:<br>Tu-whit, tu-whoo!—a merry note,<br>While greasy Joan doth keel the pot. |
| *(didascalia)* | ARMADO The words of Mercury are harsh after the [[songs]] of [[Apollo]]. You that way, we this way. Exeunt, severally |
| ADDITIONAL PASSAGES | A. The following lines found after 4.3.293 in the First Quarto represent an unrevised version of parts of Biron’s long speech, 4.3.287-341. The first six lines form the basis of 4.3.294-9; the next three are revised at 4.3.326- 30; the next four at 4.3.300-2; the last nine are less directly related to the revised version.<br>And where that you have vowed to study, lords,<br>In that each of you have forsworn his book,<br>Can you still dream, and pore, and thereon look?<br>For when would you, my lord, or you, or you,<br>Have found the ground of study’s excellence<br>Without the [[Beauty|beauty]] of a woman’s face?<br>From women’s eyes this doctrine I derive.<br>They are the ground, the books, the academes,<br>From whence doth [[Spring|spring]] the true Promethean [[Fire|fire]].<br>Why, universal plodding poisons up<br>The nimble spirits in the arteries,<br>As motion and long-during action tires<br>The sinewy vigour of the traveller.<br>Now, for not looking on a woman’s face<br>You have in that forsworn the use of eyes,<br>And study, too, the causer of your vow.<br>For where is any author in the world<br>Teaches such [[Beauty|beauty]] as a woman’s eye?<br>Learning is but an adjunct to ourself,<br>And where we are, our learning likewise is.<br>Then when ourselves we see in ladies’ eyes<br>With ourselves.<br>Do we not likewise see our learning there?<br>B. The following two lines, spoken by the Princess and found after 5.2.130 in the First Quarto, seem to represent a first draft of 5.2.131-2.<br>Hold, [[Rosaline]]. This favour thou shalt wear,<br>And then the [[King]] will [[Court|court]] thee for his dear.<br>C. The following lines found after 5.2.809 in the First Quarto represent a draft version of 5.2.824-41. |
| BIRON | And what to me, my [[Love|love]]? And what to me? |
| [[Rosaline|ROSALINE]] | You must be purged, too. Your sins are rank.<br>You are attaint with faults and perjury.<br>Therefore if you my favour mean to get<br>A twelvemonth shall you spend, and never rest<br>But seek the weary beds of people sick. |
| [[Love|LOVE]]’S LABOUR’S WON | A BRIEF ACCOUNT |
| IN | 1598, Francis Meres called as witnesses to Shakespeare’s excellence in [[Comedy|comedy]] ‘his Gentlemen of [[Verona]], his Errors, his [[Love]] Labour’s Lost, his [[Love]] Labour’s Wone, his Midsummer’s [[Night]] Dream, and his Merchant of [[Venice]]’. This was the only evidence that Shakespeare wrote a play called [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won until the discovery in 1953 of a [[Fragment|fragment]] of a bookseller’s list that had been used in the binding of a volume published in 1637/8. The [[Fragment|fragment]] itself appears to record titles sold from 9 to 17 August 1603 by a book dealer in the south of England. Among items headed ‘[inte]rludes & tragedyes’ are<br>marchant of vennis<br>taming of a shrew<br>knak to know a knave<br>knak to know an honest man<br>loves labor lost<br>loves labor won<br>No author is named for any of the items. All the plays named in the list except [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won are known to have been printed by 1600; all were written by 1596-7. Taken together, Meres’s reference in 1598 and the 1603 [[Fragment|fragment]] appear to demonstrate that a play by Shakespeare called [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won had been performed by the [[Time|time]] Meres wrote and was in print by August 1603. Conceivably the phrase served as an alternative title for one of Shakespeare’s other comedies, though the only one believed to have been written by 1598 but not listed by Meres is The Taming of the Shrew, which is named (as The Taming of A Shrew) in the bookseller’s [[Fragment|fragment]]. Otherwise we must suppose that [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won is the title of a lost play by Shakespeare, that no copy of the edition mentioned in the bookseller’s list is extant, and that Heminges and Condell failed to include it in the 1623 Folio.<br>None of these suppositions is implausible. We know of at least one other lost play attributed to Shakespeare (see [[Cardenio]], below), and of many lost works by contemporary playwrights. No copy of the first edition of [[Titus Andronicus]] was known until 1904; for I [[Henry IV]] and The Passionate [[Pilgrim]] only a [[Fragment|fragment]] of the first edition survives. And we now know that [[Troilus]] and [[Cressida]] was almost omitted from the 1623 Folio (probably for copyright reasons) despite its evident authenticity. It is also possible that, like most of the early editions of Shakespeare’s plays, the lost edition of [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won did not name him on the title-page, and this omission might go some way to explaining the failure of the edition to survive, or (if it does) to be noticed. [[Love]]’s Labour’s Won stands a much better chance of having survived, somewhere, than [[Cardenio]]: because it was printed, between 500 and 1,500 copies were once in circulation, whereas for [[Cardenio]] we know of only a single manuscript.<br>The evidence for the existence of the lost play (unlike that for [[Cardenio]]) gives us little indication of its content. Meres explicitly states, and the title implies, that it was a [[Comedy|comedy]]. Its titular pairing with [[Love]]’s Labour’s Lost suggests that they may have been written at about the same [[Time|time]]. Both Meres and the bookseller’s catalogue place it after [[Love]]’s Labour’s Lost; although neither list is necessarily chronological, Meres’s does otherwise agree with our own view of the order of composition of Shakespeare’s comedies. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1--01_exchange_dialogue" data-title="Loves Labours Lost — Act I, Scene 1 — exchange (dialogue) [1]" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Ferdinand]], [[King]] of Navarre, Biron, [[Longueville]], and [[Dumaine]] |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1--02_monologue_king" data-title="Loves Labours Lost — Act I, Scene 1 — monologue (KING) [2]" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| [[King|KING]] | Let fame, that all hunt after in their lives, Live registered upon our brazen tombs, And then grace us in the disgrace of [[Death|death]] When, spite of cormorant devouring [[Time|time]], Th’endeavour of this present breath may buy That [[Honour|honour]] which shall bate his scythe’s keen edge And make us heirs of all [[Eternity|eternity]]. Therefore, brave conquerors—for so you are, That war against your own affections And the huge army of the world’s desires— Our late edict shall strongly stand in force. Navarre shall be the [[Wonder|wonder]] of the world. Our [[Court|court]] shall be a little academe, Still and contemplative in living art. You three—Biron, [[Dumaine]], and [[Longueville]]— Have sworn for three years’ term to live with me My fellow scholars, and to keep those statutes That are recorded in this schedule here. Your oaths are passed; and now subscribe your names, That his own hand may strike his [[Honour|honour]] down That violates the smallest branch herein. If you are armed to do as sworn to do, Subscribe to your deep oaths, and keep it, too. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1--03_exchange_longueville" data-title="Loves Labours Lost — Act I, Scene 1 — exchange (LONGUEVILLE) [3]" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="intro"></span>


| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
